I haven't really thought about whether I spent too much money, had job issues, or if anyone suggested I needed emergency care.\n\nDoctor: When you were seeing or hearing things that weren’t there, were you also feeling unusually energized, sleeping much less, talking fast, or feeling especially euphoric or irritable at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I only saw and heard those things during the times when I was feeling incredibly energized, euphoric, or highly irritable. I don't recall what my sleep patterns were like or if I felt rested during those high-energy periods.\n\nDoctor: Did the seeing or hearing things and unusual beliefs themselves make it hard for you to function or lead anyone to think you needed urgent treatment or hospitalization?"
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I'm really not sure about that. I haven't thought much about whether those specific experiences on their own caused me functional problems or if they were the reason anyone mentioned hospitalization.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are an assistant analyst connecting the [Symptom Profile] with the doctor's question.\nDetermine which part of the profile (chief complaint, associated symptoms, social history, etc.)\nthe doctor's **last question** is related to.\n\n[Symptom Profile]\n▸ manic_episode\n  - Elevated Mood: A mood that is abnormally high, euphoric, and full of unmotivated happiness and vitality.\n  - Irritable Mood: A state where one gets angry or aggressive even at minor stimuli.\n  - Mood Lability: Rapid shifts in mood, alternating unpredictably between euphoria and irritability.\n  - Increased Energy: Overflowing physical and mental energy, leading to an inability to stay still and a desire to keep moving.\n  - Hyperactivity: Doing far more work than usual or starting multiple activities simultaneously.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Inability to tolerate resting or stopping, driven by a constant need to do something.\n  Duration: 7 months\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Depressed Mood: A persistently sunken mood or feeling of depression.\n  - Tearfulness: A state of frequent crying or inability to hold back tears.\n  - Sadness: A state where a sad or gloomy mood is maintained continuously.\n  - Emptiness: A feeling of inner emptiness or a state of feeling no emotion.\n  - Emotional Numbness: A state where emotions feel dulled or paralyzed.\n  - Loss of Interest: Reduced interest in previously enjoyed hobbies or leisure activities.\n  - Social Withdrawal: Reduced interest or effort in social interactions and maintaining relationships.\n  - Loss of Motivation: Lack of motivation to initiate or complete work or tasks.\n  - Anhedonia: Inability to experience positive emotions such as happiness, satisfaction, love, or pleasure.\n  - Indifference to Achievement: Insensitivity or indifference to personal achievements or success.\n  Duration: 3 months\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Depressed Mood: A persistently sunken mood or feeling of depression.\n  - Tearfulness: A state of frequent crying or inability to hold back tears.\n  - Sadness: A state where a sad or gloomy mood is maintained continuously.\n  - Emptiness: A feeling of inner emptiness or a state of feeling no emotion.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - I haven't really thought about whether I spent too much money, had job issues, or if anyone suggested I needed emergency care.\n\nDoctor: When you were seeing or hearing things that weren’t there, were you also feeling unusually energized, sleeping much less, talking fast, or feeling especially euphoric or irritable at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I only saw and heard those things during the times when I was feeling incredibly energized, euphoric, or highly irritable. Those experiences always went hand-in-hand with my high-energy episodes.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy periods, how much were you actually sleeping, and did you still feel rested even with much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I'm not really sure how many hours of sleep I was getting back then. I don't recall what my sleep patterns were like or if I felt rested during those high-energy periods.\n\nDoctor: Did the seeing or hearing things and unusual beliefs themselves make it hard for you to function or lead anyone to think you needed urgent treatment or hospitalization?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure about that. I haven't thought much about whether those specific experiences on their own caused me functional problems or if they were the reason anyone mentioned hospitalization.\n\nDoctor: During those high-energy periods, did you notice things like talking much faster than usual, your thoughts racing, feeling unusually confident or powerful, or doing risky things you normally wouldn’t do?\n\nPatient: I definitely felt an overwhelming sense of confidence and power back then, almost like I had special abilities. I also didn't really notice if I was doing risky things.\n\nDoctor: When you felt like you had special abilities, did you believe that so strongly that others said it was unrealistic or not true?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was completely convinced that I had special powers and abilities at the time. People around me kept telling me it wasn't real and that I was being unrealistic, but to me, it felt absolutely true.\n\nDoctor: Did you have one continuous seven-month period of elevated or irritable mood, or were there separate episodes with times in between when your mood returned closer to normal?"

Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.
